                                                                          Status of the strong CP problem

                                                                                         Wen-Yuan Aia,b,∗
                                                                             a State Key Laboratory of Dark Matter Physics,

                                                                      Tsung-Dao Lee Institute and School of Physics and Astronomy,

                                    Abstract

                                    The strong CP problem remains one of the major open questions in the Standard Model of particle physics. Its best-
arXiv:2609.00620v1 [hep-ph] 1 Sep 2026




                                    known solution is the Peccei–Quinn mechanism, which predicts a new pseudoscalar boson, the QCD axion. In recent
                                    years, alternative solutions and new perspectives on the foundations of the strong CP problem have also received renewed
                                    attention. In this review, we provide a pedagogical account of the underlying physics and summarise the current theoretical
                                    and phenomenological status of the problem.
                                    Keywords: strong CP problem, Yang-Mills theory, instantons, chiral symmetry breaking, QCD axions




                                         ∗ wenyuanai@sjtu.edu.cn

                                                              3
1. Introduction: what is the strong CP problem
Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) provides a remarkably successful description of the strong interactions across a wide
range of energy scales. At the fundamental level, the most general renormalisable Lagrangian consistent with gauge
invariance and Lorentz symmetry reads
                                      1                               θgs2 a e aµν
                                LM = − Gaµν Gaµν + ψ̄i iD                                                              (1.1)
                                                               
                                                        / − Mij ψj +      G G ,
                                      4                              32π 2 µν
where ψ denotes the quark multiplet in flavour space. The notation will be introduced more carefully below. In this
review, we mainly focus on the three lightest flavours, ψ = (u, d, s)T . The mass matrix M is generally complex. In the
                                                             5         5              5
diagonalised basis, it can be written as M = diag(eiα1 γ mu , eiα2 γ md , eiα3 γ ms ). Then the mass term can be written as

                           ψ̄M ψ = ψ̄L MψR + ψ̄R M† ψL ,         M = diag(eiα1 mu , eiα2 md , eiα3 ms ).               (1.2)

Although the last term in Eq. (1.1) is a total derivative at the classical level, it has physical consequences due to the
nontrivial topological structure of non-Abelian gauge theory [1, 2].
   It is important to emphasise that neither the θ parameter nor the chiral phases in the quark mass matrix are inde-
pendently physical. Under chiral field redefinitions
                                                         5                            5
                                            ψi → eiβi γ ψi ,         ψ̄i → ψ̄i eiβi γ ,                                (1.3)

we have [3, 4]
                                                                                X
                                           αi → αi + 2βi ,           θ →θ−2               βi .                         (1.4)
                                                                                  i

Therefore, only the combination
                                                       θ̄ = θ + arg det M                                              (1.5)

is invariant under chiral redefinitions and thus represents the physical strong CP phase.
   A nonzero θ̄ is widely believed to induce CP -violating effects in the strong interactions. In particular, it is expected
to generate a neutron electric dipole moment (nEDM), as well as CP-odd pion-nucleon couplings and other hadronic
observables. Experimentally, however, the neutron EDM is extremely constrained. The current bound [5, 6]

                                                       |dn | ≲ 10−26 e cm,                                             (1.6)

implies
                                                          |θ̄| ≲ 10−10 ,                                               (1.7)

which is many orders of magnitude below the “natural” expectation θ̄ ∼ O(1) in the absence of a protective mechanism.
This hierarchy constitutes the strong CP problem: why is the parameter θ̄ so small?
   The puzzle is sharpened by the existence of CP violation in the weak interactions through the Cabibbo–Kobayashi–
Maskawa (CKM) phase [7]. Within the Standard Model, there is therefore no symmetry reason that enforces an approx-
imately CP -conserving strong sector.
   From a theoretical perspective, the meaning of θ̄ is deeply tied to the structure of the QCD vacuum, the role of
topological sectors, and the interplay between chiral symmetry and axial anomalies. A proper understanding therefore
requires a careful treatment of instantons, large gauge transformations, and the emergence of the θ-vacuum, which will
be developed in the following sections.

   Conventionally, proposed resolutions of the strong CP problem fall into three broad classes:


                                                                 4
 (1) A vanishing light-quark mass: in particular, the possibility that the up-quark mass is zero, in which case θ̄ can
     be rotated away and becomes unphysical.

 (2) CP - and parity-based solutions: CP or parity is imposed as a fundamental symmetry in the ultraviolet theory
     and subsequently broken in such a way that the strong CP phase remains sufficiently small while allowing the
     observed weak-interaction CP violation.

 (3) Dynamical relaxation: the strong CP phase is promoted to a dynamical field whose potential drives it to a
     CP -conserving minimum. The most prominent realisation is the Peccei–Quinn (PQ) mechanism [8], which predicts
     the axion as a pseudo-Nambu–Goldstone boson [9, 10].

   A logically distinct question is whether the conventional connection between a nonzero θ̄ and observable strong-
interaction CP violation is itself unavoidable. Alternative viewpoints on this issue have been explored in recent years [11,
12, 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18]1 and have prompted an active debate [11, 22, 14, 16, 23, 24, 25, 26, 27, 28, 29]. The purpose
of this review is to provide a coherent and pedagogical account of the current status of the strong CP problem, from its
conceptual foundations to proposed solutions and phenomenological implications, while presenting the assumptions and
arguments underlying these alternative viewpoints in a neutral manner.
   A substantial body of review literature has developed around the strong CP problem and axion physics. Pedagogical
introductions to the strong CP problem can be found in the classic review by Cheng [30], the lectures by Peccei [31] and
Hook [32], and the recent accounts by Bonanno, Bonati and D’Elia [33] and Sannino [29]. Classic and modern reviews
of axion theory and phenomenology include Kim [34], Kim and Carosi [35], Marsh [36], Di Luzio, Giannotti, Nardi and
Visinelli [37], Choi, Im and Shin [38], and Chadha-Day, Ellis and Marsh [39]. From the perspective of low-energy hadronic
physics and EDM phenomenology, detailed reviews are provided by Pospelov and Ritz [40, 41] and by Engel, Ramsey-
Musolf and van Kolck [42]. Axion astrophysics, cosmology and experimental searches are reviewed from complementary
perspectives in Refs. [43, 44, 45, 46, 47, 48, 49, 50, 51]. For QCD topology and θ dependence, the classic review by Vicari
and Panagopoulos [52] and the recent accounts in Refs. [33, 53] provide complementary perspectives. Together, these
works cover the theoretical foundations, phenomenology, cosmology and experimental implications relevant to the strong
CP problem and QCD axion.
   The structure of this review is as follows. We begin in Part I by reviewing the theoretical origin of the strong CP
problem, from the anomalous and topological structure of QCD to the emergence of the physical phase θ̄ and its low-
energy CP -violating consequences, with particular emphasis on electric dipole moments (EDMs). In Part II, we discuss
what constitutes a viable solution to the strong CP problem and review the main conventional possibilities: a vanishing
light-quark mass, CP - and parity-based constructions, and dynamical relaxation through the PQ mechanism. Part III
develops the QCD axion in greater detail, covering its benchmark realisations and low-energy properties, cosmology,
astrophysical constraints and experimental searches, together with the axion quality problem and possible ultraviolet
resolutions. Finally, in Part IV, we revisit the theoretical foundations of the strong CP problem itself. We review recent
proposals that question the conventional interpretation of θ̄, as well as the ensuing debate, and summarise the present
status of these discussions.

  1 See also Refs. [19, 20, 21] for some other perspectives.

2. Notation
When discussing the vacuum structure of Yang–Mills theory or QCD, one often encounters instantons, which are classical
solutions of the Euclidean field equations.2 It is therefore useful to first establish our conventions for Euclidean quantities.
Following Ref. [55], we place a hat on Euclidean quantities whenever necessary to distinguish them from their Minkowskian
counterparts. Lorentz indices µ, ν, · · · take values 0, · · · , 3 in Minkowski spacetime and 1, · · · , 4 in Euclidean spacetime.
In Euclidean spacetime, there is no distinction between upper and lower indices, and we use lower indices throughout,
except for γ 5 .


2.1. QCD in Minkowski spacetime

The action in Minkowski spacetime reads (without the topological term for the moment)

where D
      / = γ µ Dµ , Gaµν = ∂µ Aaν − ∂ν Aaµ + gs f abc Abµ Acν . The structure constants f abc satisfy [T a , T b ] = if abc T c with
T a being the generators of the gauge group. QCD has gauge group SU (3)c . The topological structure relevant here is
already present in an SU (2) subgroup. For simplicity, we therefore restrict the explicit instanton calculations to SU (2),
for which f abc = εabc . The Dirac matrices γ µ satisfy {γ µ , γ ν } = 2η µν . The covariant derivative takes the form

                                                      Dµ ψi = ∂µ − igs Aaµ T a ψi                                            (2.2)
                                                                              


if ψi lives in the fundamental representation of the gauge group, and

                                                     Dµ ψi = ∂µ ψi − igs Aaµ [T a , ψi ]                                     (2.3)

if ψi lives in the adjoint representation. The subscript i on the fermions is the flavour index and throughout the paper,
we suppress the colour indices on the quark fields. In Eq. (2.1) we have not included the topological term yet. We will
see that this term will emerge from the nontrivial vacuum structure of non-Abelian gauge fields.
    Since chiral fermions lie at the centre of the discussion of the Adler-Bell-Jackiw (ABJ) anomaly [56, 57] and the strong
CP problem, it is convenient to employ the Weyl basis for the Dirac matrices:

have the properties γ 0† = γ 0 , γ i† = −γ i , {γ 5 , γ µ } = 0. The left-handed and right-handed fermions are ψL/R = 21 (1∓γ 5 )ψ,
respectively. They satisfy γ 5 ψL/R = ∓ψL/R .


2.2. QCD in Euclidean spacetime

When going from Minkowski spacetime to Euclidean spacetime, one performs the Wick rotation t → −iτ . For theories
where the metric structure does not play an essential role, this procedure is often sufficient. In Euclidean QCD, however,

Part I

The origin of the strong CP problem
3. Chiral anomaly and QCD vacuum structure

3.1. The η ′ puzzle and chiral anomaly

We begin our discussion with the chiral symmetry breaking of QCD. Consider the idealised limit M → 0 for Nf quark
flavours. The QCD Lagrangian then has the classical global symmetry

                                 U (Nf )L × U (Nf )R ≃ SU (Nf )L × SU (Nf )R × U (1)V × U (1)A ,                                      (3.1)

up to discrete identifications. The vector U (1)V factor is baryon number. The axial symmetries are spontaneously broken
by a nonvanishing quark condensate ⟨ψ̄ψ⟩ ̸= 0, leading to

                                 SU (Nf )L × SU (Nf )R × U (1)V × U (1)A → SU (Nf )V × U (1)V .                                       (3.2)

The number of Goldstone bosons equals the number of the generators of the broken symmetries. Since the quarks have
masses, the chiral symmetries are only approximate, and one then has pseudo-Goldstone bosons instead of strictly massless
Goldstone bosons. The breaking of the approximate non-Abelian axial symmetries SU (3)A accounts for the pseudoscalar
octet (π, K, η) as pseudo-Nambu–Goldstone bosons.
   If the singlet axial symmetry U (1)A were also an exact global symmetry of the quantum theory in the limit of M → 0,
one would expect a ninth pseudoscalar Nambu–Goldstone boson. This expectation is not realised in the hadron spectrum:
the η ′ is much heavier than the (π, K, η) octet states. This mismatch is the traditional U (1)A or η ′ puzzle [58]. The mass
of η ′ is incompatible with treating the singlet axial symmetry as an ordinary approximate chiral symmetry broken only
by light quark masses.
   In terms of left- and right-handed fields, a singlet axial rotation acts as

                                                   ψL → e−iβ ψL ,         ψR → eiβ ψR ,                                               (3.3)

whereas a non-singlet transformation inserts a traceless flavour generator. Classically, in the massless theory, both
transformations appear to give conserved currents. Quantum mechanically, however, only the non-singlet axial currents
remain conserved in the chiral limit. The singlet current is anomalous. This is the mechanism that removes the unwanted
ninth Goldstone boson from the low-energy spectrum and, at the same time, prepares the ground for the physical meaning
of the QCD vacuum angle [59, 60, 61, 62].
   The flavour-singlet axial current reads J 5µ = ψ̄γ µ γ 5 ψ, with the sum over the Nf light flavours understood. At the
classical level one obtains
                                                          ∂µ J 5µ = 2i ψ̄M γ 5 ψ,                                                     (3.4)

so the current is conserved when M = 0. This conclusion is modified by quantisation. With the conventions of Eq. (1.1),
the anomalous Ward identity is3
                                                                          Nf gs2 a e aµν
                                                ∂µ J5µ = 2i ψ̄M γ 5 ψ +         G G ,                                                 (3.5)
                                                                          16π 2 µν

  3 Note that Ref. [63] uses a different convention ϵ0123 = +1, which leads to an extra minus sign in the second term in Eq. (3.5).




                                                                     8
      e aµν = 1 ϵµνρσ Gaρσ . We adopt the convention
where G       2


                                                                    ϵ0123 = −1.                                                             (3.6)

The coefficient of the last term is fixed by the Adler–Bell–Jackiw anomaly and is not renormalised by strong dynamics [57,
56]. In a path-integral derivation, the same term arises from the non-invariance of the fermion measure under a chiral
change of variables [3, 4]. For a general axial current ψ̄γ µ γ 5 T ψ, the gluonic anomaly is proportional to TrT ; hence it
vanishes for traceless generators of SU (Nf )A but survives for the singlet generator.
    Equation (3.5) is often described as an explicit breaking of U (1)A , and provides the basic explanation for the absence
of the ninth Goldstone boson in the hadron spectrum. However, it does not by itself provide a microscopic picture of how
the axial charge is violated. This is the role played by instantons in gauge theory [2, 59], which we discuss next.


3.2. Topological sectors and instantons

The essential additional ingredient is the topology of non-Abelian gauge fields. This structure is most transparent in the
Euclidean path integral,
                                                          Z                                            
                                                                                 R                   ˆη
                                                                                     d4 x LE −η̄ ψ̂−ψ̄
                                                               Dψ̄ˆ Dψ̂ DÂ e
                                                                             −
                                             Z[η, η̄] =                          Ω
                                                                                                            .                               (3.7)

Here Ω denotes the spacetime volume, which is infinite for R4 . We include external fermionic sources η(x) and η̄(x) for
later use in deriving quark correlation functions.
    There are two complementary ways to understand the physical meaning of Euclidean field theory. First, the Euclidean
path integral can be regarded as a formulation of transition amplitudes in Euclidean time, which can be used to extract
quantities such as energy eigenvalues. Alternatively, it can be viewed as an analytic continuation of the Minkowski theory,
where relations derived in Euclidean spacetime are analytically continued back to Minkowski spacetime. In this review,
the second viewpoint is mostly used [59]. In both viewpoints, instantons are classical solutions of the Euclidean equations
of motion and provide the starting point for the semiclassical expansion of the Euclidean path integral.4
    In the path integral, we are concerned with finite-action gauge configurations,
                                                        Z
                                                      1
                                             SEYM =        d4 x Ĝaµν Ĝaµν < ∞.                                                            (3.8)
                                                      4 Ω

For infinite spacetime volume, Ω → ∞, the condition (3.8) implies that the field strength vanishes sufficiently rapidly at
infinity. Hence, at large Euclidean radius, the gauge potential approaches a pure gauge,

                                                               i
                                               Âµ (x) →         ω(x) ∂µ ω −1 (x)      at ∂R4 ≃ S 3 .                                       (3.9)
                                                              gs

The asymptotic gauge function ω defines a map S 3 → SU (Nc ). For Nc ≥ 2, such maps are classified by the third
homotopy gropu π3 (SU (Nc )) = Z. The corresponding integer labels disconnected classes of gauge-field configurations. In
the continuum, it is represented by the topological charge (or winding number)

                                           Z
                                      Q = d4 x q(x),        q(x) = s 2 Ĝaµν Ĝ .                                                         (3.10)
                                                                   32π

   4 Typically, after analytic continuation back to Minkowski spacetime, instantons do not correspond to real classical solutions of the equations

of motion. Nevertheless, the resulting complex saddles can still play an important role in the Minkowskian path integral, as understood within
Picard–Lefschetz theory [64, 65].


                                                                         9
With the boundary condition (3.9), Q is an integer. This gives rise to the topological quantisation of gauge-field configu-
rations. For the later discussion, we emphasise that this topological quantisation in the spacetime R4 relies on the limit
Ω → ∞.
   The topological charge labels the sectors but does not determine the configurations within them. When computing
observables through the path integral, it is useful to perform the semiclassical expansion. In the latter, the minimum-
action configurations in sectors with nonzero Q are (anti-)self-dual instanton solutions [59, 2, 54, 66]. For Q = ±1, these
are the Belavin–Polyakov–Schwartz–Tyupkin (BPST) (anti-)instantons [1], with classical Yang–Mills action
                                                                       8π 2

The anti-instanton is obtained by replacing ηaµν with the corresponding anti-self-dual symbol η̄aµν .
   For |Q| > 1, the minimum-action (anti-)self-dual configurations are described by the Atiyah–Drinfeld–Hitchin–Manin
(ADHM) construction [67]. A configuration with Q > 0 consists of a multi-instanton solution, while one with Q < 0
consists of the corresponding multi-anti-instanton solution. More generally, in the dilute instanton gas approximation
(DIGA), a sector of fixed topological charge Q may contain both instantons and anti-instantons. Denoting their numbers
by n and n̄, respectively, one has n − n̄ = Q. The (anti-)self-dual minimum-action configurations correspond to n̄ = 0
for Q > 0 and n = 0 for Q < 0, whereas generic dilute instanton configurations may contain additional instanton–anti-
instanton pairs. Each constituent carries collective coordinates describing its position, size, and gauge orientation.


3.3. Chiral charge violation in a fixed topological sector

The connection between gauge-field topology and chiral symmetry violation becomes sharp once fermions are included.
In a fixed Euclidean gauge background, the change of the chiral charge is encoded in the eigenequation of the massless
Dirac operator,

                                                         /̂ λ = λ ψλ .
                                                         Dψ                                                              (3.14)

Note that the Euclidean massless Dirac operator is anti-Hermitian and so its eigenfunctions can be readily assumed to
be orthonormal and its eigenvalues are purely imaginary λ∗ = −λ.
   Since {D,
          /̂ γ 5 } = 0, if ψλ is an eigenfunction of the massless Dirac operator with eigenvalue λ, then γ 5 ψλ is also an
eigenfunction, with eigenvalue −λ:

                                                  /̂ 5 ψλ ) = −λ(γ 5 ψλ ).
                                                  D(γ                                                                    (3.15)


                                                                  10
Thus, all nonzero eigenvalues occur in pairs of opposite sign. For any zero mode ψ0 , we may decompose it as

                                                    ψ0 = PL ψ0 + PR ψ0 ≡ ψ0L + ψ0R .                                                     (3.16)

Provided that ψ0L or ψ0R is nonvanishing, it is itself a zero mode. Therefore, the zero modes can always be chosen to be
eigenfunctions of γ 5 ,

                                                                    γ 5 ψ0 = ±ψ0 .                                                       (3.17)

We denote the numbers of right- and left-handed zero modes by nR and nL , respectively. The index theorem [68] states
that

                                             nR,i − nL,i = 2T (R) Q,             for each flavour i,                                     (3.18)

where T (R) is the Dynkin index of the gauge-group representation R. For the fundamental representation, T (R) = 1/2.
In particular, in a BPST instanton background there is one right-handed zero mode and no left-handed zero mode.5
Conversely, an anti-instanton background supports one left-handed zero mode and no right-handed zero mode.
    Below, we derive the Euclidean anomalous Ward identity for the flavour-singlet axial current. For simplicity, let us
consider Nf = 1 and assume a real mass m. We compute

                                               ∂µ Jˆµ5 = ∂µ ⟨ψ̄ˆγ̂µ γ 5 ψ̂⟩Q = −∂µ ⟨ψ̂ ψ̄ˆγ̂µ γ 5 ⟩Q .                                   (3.19)

The fermionic Green’s function SQ ≡ ⟨ψ̂(x)ψ̄ˆ(x′ )⟩Q satisfies
                                                     
                                              /̂ x + m SQ (x, y) = δ 4 (x − y).
                                              D                                                                                          (3.20)

                                                                         11
3.4. Large gauge transformations, the pre-vacuum, and the first caveat

We have seen how the chiral charge is anomalously violated in a Euclidean gauge-field background with definite topological
charge. Among the inequivalent configurations in a sector with charge Q, instantons provide the semiclassical saddle points
and thus a simple picture of the associated chiral-charge violation. Instantons are also interpreted as describing tunnelling
processes, which naturally raises the question of the corresponding initial and final states. To address this, we consider
gauge-field configurations on a spatial hypersurface at fixed time, namely the three-dimensional space R3 .
    For definiteness, we work in the temporal gauge A0 = 0. To identify the vacuum states, we first seek the classically
minimal-energy, i.e. zero-energy, configurations. These are pure gauges,

                                                                   i
                                                       Ai (x) =      ω(x)∂i ω −1 (x),                                                 (3.25)
                                                                  gs

where ω(x) is a gauge transformation matrix. If we require ω(x) to approach a direction-independent limit at spatial
infinity [69],

                                                    ω(x) → const       for   |x| → ∞,                                                 (3.26)

then R3 can be compactified to S 3 , and ω(x) defines a map S 3 → SU (Nc ). The zero-energy gauge configurations
consequently fall into different topological classes characterised by an integer, the Chern–Simons (CS) number,

                                               g2
                                                      Z                                 
                                                                  1          igs
                                   nCS [A] = s2 ϵijk d3 x Tr Ai ∂j Ak −          Ai Aj Ak ,                                           (3.27)
                                              4π                  2           3

where Ai = Aai T a and Tr(T a T b ) = δ ab /2. The pure-gauge configurations in Eq. (3.25) have nCS ∈ Z. Note that for a
general field configuration that is not pure gauge, the CS number is not necessarily an integer.
    It is important to note that Eq. (3.26) imposes a condition not only on the admissible pure-gauge configurations but
also on the admissible gauge transformations. Because of this condition, the group of admissible gauge transformations
is disconnected. In the conventional construction, two configurations related by a gauge transformation continuously
connected to the identity are regarded as gauge equivalent. Such transformations are called small gauge transformations
and form the identity component G0 . Pure-gauge configurations with the same CS number can be related by small
gauge transformations. We may therefore choose a representative configuration in each topological class and denote the
corresponding state by |nCS ⟩, where nCS labels its CS number. These states are called pre-vacua.
    Gauge transformations that are not connected to the identity are called large gauge transformations. Denoting the
full group of admissible gauge transformations by G, one has G/G0 ≃ Z, where the integer n ∈ Z the homotopy classes.
A large gauge transformation Um belonging to the component labelled by m maps

                                                         Um |nCS ⟩ = |nCS + m⟩.                                                       (3.28)

The Euclidean instanton is then interpreted semiclassically as a tunnelling trajectory between adjacent pre-vacua. More
generally, a field history interpolating between |niCS ⟩ and |nfCS ⟩ carries topological charge6

                                                             Q = nfCS − niCS .                                                        (3.29)

  6 We distinguish the winding number,    defined in the four-dimensional Euclidean spacetime, from the CS number, defined on a three-
dimensional spatial hypersurface. Unless specified otherwise, the topological charge refers to the winding number throughout this review.




                                                                     12
The gauge-redundancy caveat.               There is, however, a conceptual caveat that should be kept explicit. As noted in
Refs. [70, 71], the condition (3.26) does not have a first-principles derivation and should be regarded as an assumption.
For example, in Ref. [70], Jackiw wrote [above Eq. (36b)]

     “We shall make a very important hypothesis concerning the physically admissible finite transformations. While
     some plausible arguments can be given in support of this hypothesis (see below) in the end we must recognize
     it as an assumption, without which subsequent development cannot be made.”

Gauge transformations connected to the identity implement the Gauss-law constraint and must act trivially on physical
states. Reference [14] argues that, if the constraint (3.26) is removed, the space of gauge transformations becomes
connected and there is only one pre-vacuum;7 within this construction, gauge invariance then enforces CP symmetry in
Yang–Mills theory. For the purposes of the present part of the review, we first follow the conventional treatment. Later,
in Part IV, we return to the question of whether relaxing the constraint (3.26) may lead to different physical conclusions.


3.5. From the pre-vacuum to the θ-vacuum

The pre-vacuum state |nCS ⟩ with a definite CS number cannot represent a physical vacuum state for two reasons. First,
under a large gauge transformation it is mapped to a different state, rather than merely acquiring an overall phase.
Second, a state with fixed nCS does not satisfy the principle of cluster decomposition, which requires that sufficiently
separated experiments become statistically independent in a vacuum state [72, 73]. These considerations motivate the
introduction of the θ-vacuum.
   Since the Hamiltonian commutes with large gauge transformations, it can be diagonalised simultaneously with them.
In the basis of pre-vacua, the conventional eigenstates are
                                                      X
                                             |θ⟩ =            e−inCS θ |nCS ⟩,        0 ≤ θ < 2π,                       (3.30)
                                                     nCS ∈Z

up to an overall normalisation. With the convention above,

                                                                U1 |θ⟩ = eiθ |θ⟩.                                       (3.31)

|θ⟩ is called the θ-vacuum. Different values of θ label superselection sectors in the idealised infinite-volume theory: local
gauge-invariant operators cannot change the eigenvalue of U1 . In this sense, the classical Yang–Mills Lagrangian does not
select a particular value of θ; rather, θ specifies an additional parameter of the quantum theory.
   The equivalence between Eq. (3.30) and the θ term in the Lagrangian can be understood by inserting complete sets
of pre-vacua into the Euclidean transition amplitude. If ⟨nfCS |e−HT |niCS ⟩ receives contributions from field histories with
Q = nfCS − niCS , then

                                                       Q∈Z

where N is a common normalisation factor and can be absorbed into the path integral measure. This corresponds to
adding a topological term to the Lagrangian,

4. The ’t Hooft vertex and fermionic correlation functions

operator.

                                                                                    16
4.2. Fermionic correlation functions and the second caveat

As an illustrative semiclassical approach, the DIGA approximates the relevant gauge-field configurations by dilute ensem-
bles of n instantons and n̄ anti-instantons, labelled by the pair (n, n̄). In a weakly coupled theory, or at short distances
where asymptotic freedom renders the gauge coupling small, the semiclassical expansion around individual instantons
provides a controlled framework for understanding how different topological sectors contribute to the path integral [2, 55].
In low-energy QCD at zero temperature, however, large instantons are not parametrically suppressed, and the DIGA
should therefore not be regarded as a quantitatively reliable description of the physical QCD vacuum in the infrared.
What is robust is not the DIGA itself, but the existence of topologically nontrivial sectors and the possibility that the
path integral receives contributions from all integer values of Q [69, 33, 53]. In the derivation below, we adopt the sim-
plest version of the DIGA, in which instantons and anti-instantons are treated as independent. Extensions incorporating
interactions between instantons and anti-instantons have been studied extensively; see, for example, Refs. [74, 66].
   For simplicity, we consider Nf = 1. We allow a general chiral phase associated with the mass, M = m exp(iαγ 5 ) with
m > 0. The relevant quark correlation function is given by

by the ‘t Hooft zero modes, sufficiently far away from the points x0,ν and x0,ν̄ the Green’s function is given by the form
in the background without instantons, i.e.

We argue that the minus sign on the right-hand side should be absent. With a common regulator and a consistent choice

in the numerator and denominator and cancels completely in their ratio. Finally, we note that both ϖ and Θ have the
same value for an instanton and an anti-instanton.

The first term represents the instanton-induced contribution. Recalling that S0 (x, x′ ) contains the chiral phase e−iαγ ,
as shown in Eq. (4.22), the correlation function above would correspond to Eq. (4.16), which leads to CP violation.
   From Eq. (4.32), one may infer the energy density of the θ-vacuum,

                                                     ρ(θ) = −2κ cos(α + θ),                                           (4.34)

which has a minimum at α + θ = 0. Since α + θ is a theory parameter instead of a dynamical field, there is no reason for
it to choose a value that minimises the energy.
   The derivations of Eqs. (4.32) and (4.33) can be straightforwardly generalised to Nf flavours, see Ref. [11]. Note that,
since now there is no minus sign in Eq. (4.25), for Nf flavours one would simply get ρ(θ) = −2κNf cos(ᾱ + θ), while the
incorrect Eq. (4.26) would give ρ(θ) = −2κNf cos(ᾱ + θ + Nf π).


The order-of-limits caveat.        In the calculation above, we have kept Ω finite throughout and taken the thermodynamic
limit Ω → ∞ only at the end. However, as emphasised below Eq. (3.10), topological quantisation itself arises only in
the infinite-spacetime-volume limit for R4 . This observation motivates an alternative prescription in which one first
takes Ω → ∞ within each topological sector and only subsequently sums over the sectors to infinity. Reference [11] has
demonstrated the non-commutativity of the order of taking these two limits and shown that the alternative order of
limits can lead to a form for the fermion correlation functions where the CP -odd phases can be removed, corresponding
to Eq. (4.17). Other authors have defended the conventional approach [23, 26, 25, 28]. The present review will not
assume that this issue is resolved by fiat. Instead, we will separate the standard framework, used throughout most
phenomenology, from the later conceptual reassessment.


4.3. Chiral perturbation theory

The DIGA discussed above provides an intuitive microscopic picture of the anomalous breaking of U (1)A and of the
dependence on the vacuum angle. At zero temperature and low energies, however, the dilute instanton gas is not
quantitatively controlled. A more systematic description of the infrared dynamics is provided by chiral perturbation
theory (ChPT) [77, 78], which is based on the pattern of spontaneous chiral symmetry breaking and organises low-
energy observables as an expansion in a generic soft momentum, conventionally denoted by p, and in the light-quark


                                                                  19
masses [79, 80]. In the conventional formulation of QCD, ChPT provides a controlled description of the dependence of
the vacuum energy and low-energy observables on θ̄ without assuming a dilute ensemble of instantons [81, 82, 83, 37]. As
we shall see, however, it is useful to distinguish the constraints imposed by chiral symmetry and spurion analysis from
the matching of the effective theory to the underlying QCD generating functional.
    We first formulate the theory for a general number Nf of light quark flavours. Temporarily neglecting the U (1)A
anomaly, the approximate symmetry U (Nf )L ×U (Nf )R is spontaneously broken to U (Nf )V , up to discrete identifications.
The corresponding pseudo-Nambu–Goldstone fields can be collected into a matrix U (x) ∈ U (Nf ). Ignoring the anomalous
singlet interaction for the moment, the leading-order chiral Lagrangian is [79, 80]10

                                               F02                 F 2 B0
                                       L(2)        Tr ∂µ U ∂ µ U † + 0     Tr MU + U † M† ,                                           (4.35)
                                                                                         
                                        χ =
                                                4                     2

where F0 denotes the pseudoscalar decay constant in the chiral limit, while B0 is related to the magnitude of the quark
condensate through ⟨ψ̄jL ψiR ⟩ = −(F02 B0 /2)⟨Uij ⟩.11 The mesonic field carries the same chiral quantum numbers as
Uij ∼ ψ̄jL ψiR and therefore transforms as U → RU L† under U (Nf )L × U (Nf )R . Correspondingly, the mass matrix is
treated as a spurion transforming as M → LMR† , so that Tr(MU ) is invariant.
    The anomalous breaking of the singlet axial symmetry must be incorporated separately. A particularly transparent way
of encoding its symmetry structure is to introduce a determinantal interaction, motivated by the Kobayashi–Maskawa–’t
Hooft interaction [84, 85, 2],

                                              ∆Lanom = |λ|F04 e−iξ det U + eiξ det U † ,                                              (4.36)
                                                                                      


where ξ denotes the phase carried by the corresponding low-energy coupling. This interaction preserves SU (Nf )L ×
SU (Nf )R × U (1)V while breaking the continuous U (1)A symmetry, and provides a simple way of keeping track of the
anomalous singlet phase.
                                                                                                                         5
    The relevant spurionic symmetry can now be made explicit. Under an axial transformation ψi → eiβγ ψi , the effective
theory is invariant if the fields and spurions are simultaneously transformed according to

                                        M → e−2iβ M,            U → e2iβ U,        θ → θ + 2Nf β.                                     (4.37)

Since ᾱ ≡ arg det M, the first transformation implies ᾱ → ᾱ − 2Nf β. Invariance of Eq. (4.36) further requires ξ →
ξ + 2Nf β. This transformation property is satisfied by both of the assignments [11, 16, 24]
                                             
                                             θ,      (conventional matching),
                                             
                                        ξ=                                                                                            (4.38)
                                             −ᾱ, (alternative matching).
                                             


Thus, the spurion transformation properties by themselves do not distinguish between these two possibilities. The value
of ξ must ultimately be fixed by matching the low-energy effective theory to the underlying QCD generating functional
or, equivalently, to an appropriate set of QCD correlation functions.

  10 In our convention, M is defined through ψ̄ Mψ + h.c., while some references instead define the mass matrix through ψ̄ Mψ + h.c..

generating functional, in which the quarks are the dynamical fields, and of the corresponding low-energy generating functional, in which U is
the dynamical field, with respect to M, and then matching the two results yields the stated relation.

   It is sometimes argued that ChPT by itself is sufficient to establish the presence of CP violation. Such an argument,

mass matrix be diagonal, M = diag(m1 eiα1 , . . . , mNf eiαNf ), and parameterise the vacuum orientation of the chiral
condensate as
                                                                                  

When the anomalous singlet interaction dominates over the explicit quark-mass terms, as is appropriate when the singlet

                                                                                            P

suppressed by the light-quark masses relative to the anomalous singlet scale. Consequently, the residual phase controlling
CP violation in the low-energy theory is      i (αi + ϕi ) ≈ ᾱ + ξ. For the conventional choice ξ = θ, this reduces to
                                            P

θ̄ = θ + ᾱ, whereas for ξ = −ᾱ it vanishes. Correspondingly, CP -odd observables are controlled by this residual phase
and, sufficiently close to the CP -conserving point, are odd functions of it and begin linearly in its expansion.
   To make this point more transparent, we can perform an anomalous singlet axial rotation to transfer the topological
angle into the quark mass matrix, followed by non-singlet axial rotations that distribute the resulting total phase uniformly
among the flavours. In this basis, one has

    The same issue can be expressed in the large-Nc formulation in which an auxiliary field q(x) is introduced to represent
the topological charge density [86],

                  F02                   F 2 B0                   i                          Nc 2

functions. With the order of limits advocated in Refs. [11, 24], this matching leads to ξ = −ᾱ. The point of disagreement
is therefore not the chiral operator content itself, but the matching of the anomalous singlet phase to the underlying QCD
generating functional.

  12 Upon identifying q(x) with the topological charge density, q(x) =        gs

                                                                                          e µν , it may appear natural to identify the coefficient of

q(x) in the effective theory directly with the fundamental parameter θ. Such an identification, however, does not follow from the correspondence
between the operators alone. More generally, parameters multiplying operators in an effective theory are matching coefficients: after degrees
of freedom are integrated out, they can encode quantum effects from the underlying theory and are fixed by requiring the effective theory to
reproduce appropriate correlation functions or observables of the fundamental theory. Consequently, the coefficient multiplying the effective
operator q(x) must ultimately be determined by matching, rather than inferred solely from the appearance of the corresponding term in the
ultraviolet Lagrangian. Furthermore, the EFT action is minimised with respect to variations of q(x), so that Ω d4 x q(x) is not subject to a
                                                                                                            R

global topological constraint, unlike in the underlying QCD theory.

quark masses. In particular, if any quark were exactly massless, m∗ would vanish and the vacuum energy would become
independent of θ̄, consistently with the fact that the vacuum angle can then be removed by an exact chiral rotation [81, 82].

in agreement with Eq. (4.51). In contrast to the simple cosine dependence obtained in the DIGA, Eq. (4.55) exhibits the

quantitative description of the zero-temperature QCD vacuum.
   A noteworthy special case occurs at θ̄ = π. Since CP sends θ̄ → −θ̄ and the theory is 2π periodic in θ̄, this is itself a
CP -symmetric point. At θ̄ = π, Eq. (4.52) reduces to

                                            V (φ, π) = −F02 B0 (md − mu ) sin φ.                                      (4.57)

For the physical ordering md > mu , the leading-order vacuum therefore lies at φ = π/2. Naively, CP acts as φ → −φ
together with θ̄ → −θ̄. However, identifying −π with π in the parametrisation of the mass term Mθ̄ U
                                                                                                   e +h.c. is accompanied

by the shift φ → φ + π. Thus, within the theory at θ̄ = π, the effective action of CP is φ → π − φ, under which the
vacuum at φ = π/2 is invariant. The vacuum therefore preserves CP for nondegenerate physical quark masses. In the
isospin-symmetric limit mu = md = m, by contrast, the leading-order potential becomes flat in φ at θ̄ = π and cannot
by itself determine the vacuum structure. Higher-order chiral terms, or a formulation retaining the anomalous singlet
dynamics explicitly, lift this accidental degeneracy and can yield two isolated, degenerate vacua related by CP . Choosing


                                                                23
either of them then spontaneously breaks CP , giving rise to the Dashen phenomenon [87, 82, 88]. More complete analyses
show that spontaneous CP breaking at θ̄ = π can persist over a finite range of nondegenerate quark masses around the
isospin-symmetric limit [89], although the physical QCD quark masses lie outside this region.
   ChPT is systematically improvable. Higher-order corrections arise from loops generated by Eq. (4.44) and from
higher-order operators in the chiral expansion accompanied by additional low-energy constants [79, 80]. Such corrections
are important for precision calculations of the topological susceptibility, the QCD axion mass, and other quantities that
depend on the θ̄-dependent vacuum energy [83, 37]. In the SU (2) theory, the strange quark is integrated out and its
effects are absorbed into the low-energy constants, while the SU (3) theory treats the kaons and the η as explicit degrees
of freedom. The two descriptions are useful in complementary regimes.
   It is worth stressing the logical status of this analysis in the context of the present review. Equations (4.50) and (4.55)
are the standard ChPT results obtained after matching the low-energy theory to the conventional θ̄-dependent QCD
generating functional. Chiral symmetry and spurion analysis determine the allowed operator structures and their trans-
formation properties, but do not by themselves determine all matching coefficients and phases. The recent debate
concerns whether the conventional matching ξ = θ is uniquely implied by QCD, or whether a different prescription for
defining the infinite-volume path integral leads to the alternative matching ξ = −ᾱ while respecting the same spurionic
symmetries [11, 16, 23, 24]. We return to this underlying conceptual question in Part IV.
   The same vacuum alignment that determines the θ̄-dependent vacuum energy also fixes the CP -odd interactions
among the low-energy hadronic degrees of freedom. In the conventional framework, expanding the chiral effective theory
around the aligned vacuum generates CP -odd mesonic and baryonic interactions proportional to θ̄ and to the light-quark
masses. We turn to these physical consequences in Sec. 5.



5. CP -violating effects from a nonzero θ̄

5.1. CP -odd hadronic interactions in chiral effective theory

Throughout this section, we adopt the conventional matching discussed in Sec. 4.3, for which the low-energy theory
depends on the invariant angle θ̄. A nonzero θ̄ then gives rise to CP -odd hadronic interactions. These effects were first
analysed using current algebra and early chiral methods [90, 81], and were subsequently formulated more systematically
within chiral effective theory [91, 92, 93]; see also Refs. [40, 42, 41, 29] for reviews and recent discussions.
   The vacuum alignment derived in the previous section is important not only for determining the vacuum energy,
but also for identifying the CP -odd interactions. After expanding around the aligned vacuum, the quark-mass matrix
contains the phases (θ̄/Nf + ϕi ), so that its pseudoscalar component for each flavour is proportional to mi sin(θ̄/Nf + ϕi ).
For sufficiently small θ̄, Eq. (4.49) gives mi sin(θ̄/Nf + ϕi ) = m∗ θ̄ + O(θ̄3 ), independently of the flavour index at leading
order. At the quark level, the resulting CP -odd mass term can therefore be written, up to an overall sign convention, as
a flavour-singlet pseudoscalar operator proportional to m∗ θ̄ i ψ̄i iγ 5 ψi [90, 81, 92]. Since this term originates from the
                                                             P

same quark-mass spurion as the ordinary scalar mass term, chiral symmetry relates several CP -odd low-energy constants
to CP -even quantities governing the quark-mass dependence of hadronic observables.
   Before turning to the baryon sector, it is useful to illustrate explicitly how a CP -odd hadronic interaction emerges
after vacuum alignment. For simplicity, consider two light flavours and temporarily retain the U (1)A singlet pseudoscalar




                                                              24
rather than integrating it out. We write

The anomalous singlet interaction determines the singlet mass and participates in the vacuum alignment, but does not

is converted into a physical CP -odd hadronic interaction after vacuum alignment. The corresponding CP -violating
η (′) → ππ amplitudes have been studied within chiral effective theory in Ref. [91].
    For EDM phenomenology, the most important low-energy interactions involve baryons. ChPT can be extended to
include the nucleon doublet N = (p, n)T ; at momenta well below the nucleon mass, it is particularly convenient to
employ heavy-baryon ChPT [94, 95]. The leading nonderivative CP -odd pion-nucleon interactions can be decomposed
into isoscalar, isovector, and isotensor structures [92, 96, 97, 42],

                                      LCP                             0              0

                                                                                             
                                       πN = ḡ0 N̄ τ · πN + ḡ1 N̄ N π + ḡ2 N̄ 3τ3 π − τ · π N + · · · ,


where ḡ0 , ḡ1 , and ḡ2 multiply isospin tensors of rank 0, 1, and 2, respectively, conventionally referred to as the ∆I = 0, 1, 2
interactions.14 These couplings connect the underlying CP -violating quark interaction to both single-nucleon and nuclear
observables. They generate long-distance contributions to nucleon electric dipole form factors through pion loops and, in
nuclei, long-range CP -odd forces through one-pion exchange.
    For the θ̄ term, the leading CP -odd quark-mass interaction is an isoscalar under SU (2)V . It can therefore generate
ḡ0 without additional isospin breaking. By contrast, ḡ1 requires an insertion proportional to md − mu and is suppressed
in the isospin limit, while the isotensor coupling ḡ2 arises only at still higher order in isospin breaking and is generally
negligible for the θ̄ source at the accuracy relevant for present phenomenology [92, 93, 97]. Consequently, ḡ0 is the

dominant CP -odd pion-nucleon coupling induced by θ̄, although ḡ1 can be important in observables for which the ḡ0
contribution is suppressed by spin-isospin selection rules.
   A particularly useful consequence of chiral symmetry is the relation between ḡ0 and the strong neutron-proton mass
splitting. The CP -odd quark-mass interaction induced by θ̄ and the isospin-breaking mass term proportional to md − mu
arise from the same chiral-symmetry-breaking spurion, so that their leading hadronic realisations involve the same low-
energy constant. Defining m̄ = (mu +md )/2, ϵ = (md −mu )/(mu +md ), and δmstr
                                                                           N = (mn −mp )QCD , one obtains [81, 92, 93]

in the convention of Eq. (5.6). In the two-flavour limit, m∗ = m̄(1 − ϵ2 )/2, so that ḡ0 = −[δmstr
                                                                                                N /(4Fπ )][(1 − ϵ )/ϵ]θ̄ + · · · .
                                                                                                                 2


This relation replaces the direct determination of a CP -odd hadronic matrix element by the CP -even strong neutron-
proton mass splitting, which is accessible to lattice QCD. In SU (3) ChPT the relation is preserved by loop corrections
through next-to-next-to-leading order, while the local terms that violate it are expected to induce only few-percent
corrections [93]. Using updated lattice inputs, a recent analysis finds [93, 98, 99]

                                                    ḡ0 ≃ −17.2(2.0) × 10−3 θ̄.                                                (5.8)

   The coupling ḡ1 is less precisely known. Although it can be related at leading order to isospin-breaking quantities,
higher-order contributions include an independent short-distance low-energy constant, substantially weakening the pre-
dictive power of the relation. The commonly used two-flavour estimate, including an estimate of this short-distance
contribution, is [100, 99]

                                                      ḡ1 ≃ 3.4(1.5) × 10−3 θ̄,                                                (5.9)

with an uncertainty of roughly 50%.
   The pion-nucleon couplings do not, however, exhaust the leading low-energy information required for nucleon EDMs.
Chiral effective theory also contains local CP -odd photon-nucleon operators that encode unresolved short-distance dy-
namics. In a relativistic notation convenient for the discussion below, they may be written as [101, 92, 102]

                                              i  ¯r       ¯r
                                      LCP                                                                                     (5.10)
                                                                    µν 5

where µ is the renormalisation scale, and d¯0r and d¯1r are the renormalised isoscalar and isovector low-energy constants,
respectively. These coefficients encode the response to the θ̄ source arising from QCD dynamics at scales not resolved
explicitly in the chiral theory. They therefore do not represent independent sources of CP violation: in the present frame-
work, where θ̄ is the only CP -violating parameter, d¯0,1
                                                      r
                                                          must vanish for θ̄ = 0 and are odd functions of θ̄. Consequently, for
|θ̄| ≪ 1, they are linear in θ̄. Unlike ḡ0 , these short-distance coefficients are not fixed by a corresponding relation to a single
CP -even hadronic observable, because electromagnetic interactions allow additional independent chiral structures [92].
Although the local operators occur at a higher chiral index than the leading ḡ0 interaction, their tree-level contributions
to the nucleon EDM are of the same chiral order as the leading pion-loop contribution generated by ḡ0 [101, 102]. The
coefficients d¯0,1
               r
                   must therefore be treated, together with the CP -odd pion-nucleon couplings, as leading hadronic inputs
for the nucleon EDM. Their interplay with the long-distance pion contribution will be discussed in Sec. 5.2.
   For completeness, three-flavour SU (3) ChPT also generates a CP -odd η-nucleon interaction. With the convention

                                                     LCP

the leading coupling can be related to the light- and strange-quark nucleon sigma terms [93],
                                                                     
                                                   2m∗ θ̄ σN s   σN l

where σN q = mq ⟨N |q̄q|N ⟩ and σN l = σN u + σN d . This relation is less precise than the corresponding one for ḡ0 , with
higher-order SU (3)-breaking effects estimated at roughly the 30% level [93]. The coupling ḡ0η contributes to hadronic
observables such as the nucleon EDM. In nuclear systems, however, the typical momentum transfer is well below mη , so
that η exchange is effectively short-ranged. Upon matching onto an SU (2) nuclear effective field theory (EFT) in which
the η is integrated out, its contribution is absorbed into CP -odd nucleon-nucleon contact interactions.
   The interactions displayed above are the most relevant ones for the discussion that follows, but they do not exhaust
the CP -odd chiral Lagrangian. Higher-order terms include derivative pion-nucleon interactions, purely pionic operators,
short-range CP -odd nucleon-nucleon interactions, and additional electromagnetic operators [92, 96, 97]. Their relative
importance depends on the chiral and isospin transformation properties of the underlying source. In particular, higher-
dimensional CP -violating quark and gluon operators generally produce a hierarchy of hadronic low-energy constants
different from that induced by the QCD θ̄ term [96, 97, 41].


5.2. Nucleon electric dipole moments

Among the hadronic observables induced by a nonzero θ̄, the EDMs of the neutron and proton provide particularly direct
probes of CP violation. The neutron EDM has played a central role in quantifying the strong CP problem since the
earliest analyses [90, 81]. The relation between the underlying θ̄ term and the nucleon EDMs has subsequently been
studied using chiral effective theory [91, 103, 101, 104, 102, 105], QCD sum rules [106], and lattice QCD [107, 108, 109];
see also Refs. [40, 42, 110, 41] for reviews.
   For an on-shell spin-1/2 nucleon, the CP -odd coupling to a single photon is parametrized by the electric dipole form
factor (EDFF), F3,N (q 2 ) [101, 102, 105]. A convenient decomposition of the electromagnetic-current matrix element is

The separation between local and loop contributions is renormalisation-scheme and scale dependent, whereas the physical
combinations d0 and d1 are independent of µ. The significance of the pion loops is that they contain nonanalytic
dependence on the light-quark masses and momentum that cannot be mimicked by local operators and therefore represents
genuine long-distance information [81, 101, 102].
    At leading one-loop order and in the isospin-symmetric limit, the nonanalytic pion contribution is purely isovector.
It arises from one insertion of the axial coupling gA and one insertion of the CP -odd coupling ḡ0 [81, 101, 102]. In the
convention of Eq. (5.6), the renormalised loop contribution contains the leading nonanalytic term

The coefficient of the logarithmic dependence on mπ is fixed by chiral symmetry, while the µ dependence is cancelled
by that of the renormalised short-distance coefficient d¯1r (µ). At subleading order, recoil and isospin-breaking corrections
modify the leading isovector result, and the EDFF develops additional nonanalytic structure, including an isoscalar
contribution [104, 102]. Thus, ChPT predicts the characteristic long-distance quark-mass and momentum dependence
of the nucleon EDFF, but not the complete static EDMs: the latter also depend on the independent short-distance
coefficients d¯0,1
               r
                   and therefore require additional nonperturbative input.
    As an illustration, a covariant baryon-ChPT analysis used lattice-QCD data to constrain the relevant short-distance
low-energy constants and obtained nucleon EDMs of order 10−16 θ̄ e cm at the physical point [105]. Ultimately, however,
a first-principles determination of the complete nucleon EDM requires nonperturbative QCD.
    Direct lattice simulations at a real nonzero θ̄ are complicated by the complex Euclidean weight. In the phenomeno-
logically relevant regime |θ̄| ≪ 1, one can instead expand around the CP -conserving theory,

where ⟨Q⟩0 = 0 has been used. The EDM may then be extracted from the CP -odd form factor F3 (q 2 ) or, alternatively,
from the spin-dependent energy shift in a background electric field. Important systematic uncertainties include topological
sampling, excited-state contamination, finite-volume and discretisation effects, and the extrapolations to the physical
quark masses and, for form-factor calculations, to q 2 = 0 [110].
    Representative determinations of the θ̄-induced nucleon EDMs are summarised in Table 5.1. For ease of comparison,
all results are expressed in units of 10−3 θ̄ e fm. The first two direct lattice determinations in Table 5.1 (from Refs. [107,
108]) give very similar neutron central values, whereas the more recent background-field result is substantially larger in
magnitude. In Ref. [109], the quoted systematic uncertainty includes excited-state and background-field effects, while
discretisation, finite-volume, and chiral-extrapolation uncertainties have not yet been fully quantified. A precision first-
principles determination of dn /θ̄ therefore remains an active problem.
    Experimentally, the strongest direct nucleon-EDM constraint is provided by the neutron. The current measurement
gives [6]

                                            dn = (0.0 ± 1.1stat ± 0.2sys ) × 10−26 e cm,                                         (5.20)

corresponding to

                                             |dn | < 1.8 × 10−26 e cm       (90% C.L.).                                          (5.21)

Using, for example, the result of Ref. [108] gives the representative constraint [99]

                                                 |θ̄| ≲ 1.2 × 10−10      (90% C.L.),                                             (5.22)

although the precise conversion depends on the nonperturbative determination of dn /θ̄. It is therefore appropriate at
present to summarise the neutron-EDM implication as the order-of-magnitude constraint |θ̄| ≲ 10−10 .
    The nucleon EDMs also constitute important one-body contributions to the EDMs of composite nuclei, while the
CP -odd pion-nucleon couplings introduced in Eq. (5.6) generate long-range CP -odd nuclear forces. Their interplay is
discussed in the next subsection.


5.3. Nuclear, atomic, and molecular electric dipole moments

The effects of a nonzero θ̄ are not restricted to individual nucleons. The hadronic interactions discussed in Secs. 5.1
and 5.2, in particular the intrinsic nucleon EDMs and the CP -odd pion-nucleon couplings, induce CP -odd nuclear forces
and moments, which in turn generate observable effects in atomic and molecular systems. EDM measurements across
these different systems therefore provide complementary probes of hadronic CP violation [42, 111, 112, 113, 114, 115].


                                                                  29
   We begin with nuclear EDMs. At low energies, the relevant hadronic ingredients are the intrinsic nucleon EDMs,
the CP -odd pion-nucleon couplings ḡ0,1,2 introduced in Eq. (5.6), and short-range CP -odd nucleon-nucleon interactions.
In chiral EFT, the leading long-range CP -odd nuclear force is generated by one-pion exchange involving one ordinary
CP -even pion-nucleon vertex and one CP -odd vertex [92, 96, 97]. Short-range effects are encoded in independent nucleon-
nucleon contact operators. For the QCD θ̄ term, these contact interactions enter at higher chiral order and are expected to
be subleading to one-pion exchange, whereas their relative importance can be substantially different for other microscopic
sources of CP violation [96, 97, 93].
   It is useful to separate the nuclear EDM schematically into

                                                    (N )            (2b)
                                             dA = dA       + dpol
                                                              A + dA       + ··· ,                                   (5.23)

       (N )

                                                 (2b)

and other subleading corrections [97, 111]. For a nucleus polarised along the z axis, with an unperturbed ground state
|Ψ0 ⟩ of definite parity, the leading one-body and polarization contributions may be written schematically as
